Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness, even when you’re not actively doing something, like a feeling that you can’t quite settle down even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more noticeable when I’m supposed to be still. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I sort of catch myself and think, “Right, settle down.”\n\nIt’s not as if I’m trying to be disruptive. It just feels like my body wants to keep doing something, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like preparing a report or reading something lengthy, even if there aren’t any obvious distractions around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks or conversations, or do you get distracted easily by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main issue, honestly. I mean, everyone gets distracted sometimes, don’t they? In conversations I can usually follow along, especially at work. If there’s a lot going on around me I might notice it, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness, even when you’re not actively doing something, like a feeling that you can’t quite settle down even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more noticeable when I’m supposed to be still. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I sort of catch myself and think, “Right, settle down.”\n\nIt’s not as if I’m trying to be disruptive. It just feels like my body wants to keep doing something, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like preparing a report or reading something lengthy, even if there aren’t any obvious distractions around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. With school work, if I need to prepare something or read through material, I usually can do it, especially if I know it has to be done.\n\nI mean, I may prefer to break things up a bit rather than sit for hours, but I wouldn’t want to say I can’t focus. That sounds stronger than what I experience, I think. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden feelings of anxiety or panic, like your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're overwhelmed by a sense of fear?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get worried or tense like anyone, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I wouldn’t describe it as panic or anything like that.\n\nI don’t really have episodes where my heart is racing or I can’t breathe from fear. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I still enjoy teaching, for the most part, and I like spending time with my family. Hobbies too, when I actually make time for them.\n\nOf course I can get tired or feel like I’d rather have a quiet evening, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or that things don’t give me pleasure anymore. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move is something that other people might notice, like fidgeting, pacing, or an inability to sit still for more than a few moments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it might be noticeable at times, though I hope it’s not too disruptive. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or somewhere like church where I’m meant to be still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around in the seat.\n\nUsually I notice and sort of tell myself, “Settle down,” and then I try to stop. It’s not that I’m jumping up constantly, but yes, I can be a bit fidgety. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt so agitated or restless that other people could tell you were unusually tense, or where you were pacing, fidgeting, or moving around much more than usual in a way that wasn’t normal for you?\n\nPatient: Not really “agitated” in a big way, no… but yes, I suppose there are times people could notice I’m a bit restless.\n\nLike when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or even in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Sometimes it gets enough that I have to sort of remind myself, “Come on, settle down.”\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s extreme, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness or need to move ever caused you any problems with your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me if I’m fidgeting or I seem like I want to move things along. And in my community—like book club—it’s similar. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, but I’ll leave feeling embarrassed, like I didn’t come across well.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it has affected relationships a bit. Not terribly, but enough that I notice it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move has been present most days, and has been going on for at least several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d say every single day, but most days, yes, it’s there in some form.\n\nIt’s been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, I’d say—since my youngest left for college and the house got quieter. I suppose I’ve been more aware of it since then. It’s not constant or anything, but yes, it’s been going on for more than a few weeks. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I seem to go from lesson planning to putting in laundry, to helping with softball practice, to chopping vegetables for some community event… without much of a pause.\n\nIt’s not frantic, exactly. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic. But even on evenings when I’ve promised myself I’m going to rest, I somehow end up moving from one thing to the next. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks or conversations, or do you get distracted easily by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main issue, honestly. I mean, everyone gets distracted sometimes, don’t they? In conversations I can usually follow along, especially at work. If there’s a lot going on around me I might notice it, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness, even when you’re not actively doing something, like a feeling that you can’t quite settle down even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more noticeable when I’m supposed to be still. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I sort of catch myself and think, “Right, settle down.”\n\nIt’s not as if I’m trying to be disruptive. It just feels like my body wants to keep doing something, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like preparing a report or reading something lengthy, even if there aren’t any obvious distractions around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. With school work, if I need to prepare something or read through material, I usually can do it, especially if I know it has to be done.\n\nI mean, I may prefer to break things up a bit rather than sit for hours, but I wouldn’t want to say I can’t focus. That sounds stronger than what I experience, I think. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden feelings of anxiety or panic, like your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're overwhelmed by a sense of fear?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get worried or tense like anyone, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I wouldn’t describe it as panic or anything like that.\n\nI don’t really have episodes where my heart is racing or I can’t breathe from fear. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I still enjoy teaching, for the most part, and I like spending time with my family. Hobbies too, when I actually make time for them.\n\nOf course I can get tired or feel like I’d rather have a quiet evening, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or that things don’t give me pleasure anymore. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move is something that other people might notice, like fidgeting, pacing, or an inability to sit still for more than a few moments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it might be noticeable at times, though I hope it’s not too disruptive. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or somewhere like church where I’m meant to be still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around in the seat.\n\nUsually I notice and sort of tell myself, “Settle down,” and then I try to stop. It’s not that I’m jumping up constantly, but yes, I can be a bit fidgety. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt so agitated or restless that other people could tell you were unusually tense, or where you were pacing, fidgeting, or moving around much more than usual in a way that wasn’t normal for you?\n\nPatient: Not really “agitated” in a big way, no… but yes, I suppose there are times people could notice I’m a bit restless.\n\nLike when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or even in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Sometimes it gets enough that I have to sort of remind myself, “Come on, settle down.”\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s extreme, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness or need to move ever caused you any problems with your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me if I’m fidgeting or I seem like I want to move things along. And in my community—like book club—it’s similar. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, but I’ll leave feeling embarrassed, like I didn’t come across well.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it has affected relationships a bit. Not terribly, but enough that I notice it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move has been present most days, and has been going on for at least several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d say every single day, but most days, yes, it’s there in some form.\n\nIt’s been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, I’d say—since my youngest left for college and the house got quieter. I suppose I’ve been more aware of it since then. It’s not constant or anything, but yes, it’s been going on for more than a few weeks. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself starting tasks without thinking them through, interrupting others, or feeling like you're moving or speaking too quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I’m a bit embarrassed to admit it.\n\nIn group discussions, like at book club or in curriculum meetings, I’ll sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. It’s not that I mean to be rude—I really don’t—but the answer or response is already there in my head, and before I’ve properly checked myself, I’ve said it.\n\nI do try to catch it and apologize, but yes, that does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

  }

Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in to see me?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s probably not a huge thing, but I’ve noticed I’m having trouble just… staying seated when I’m supposed to. Like in staff meetings, or even at dinner, I’ll suddenly get up to refill coffee, check a handout, walk to the back of the room—little things like that. It feels like I need to move, even though nothing is actually urgent. I’m not trying to be rude, of course, but I suppose it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling restless or needing to move even when you're not sitting, like during conversations or while working on tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose I do, though I’ve always told myself it’s just being productive. Since the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I seem to go from lesson planning to putting in laundry, to helping with softball practice, to chopping vegetables for some community event… without much of a pause.\n\nIt’s not frantic, exactly. I don’t mean to make it sound dramatic. But even on evenings when I’ve promised myself I’m going to rest, I somehow end up moving from one thing to the next. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on tasks or conversations, or do you get distracted easily by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s the main issue, honestly. I mean, everyone gets distracted sometimes, don’t they? In conversations I can usually follow along, especially at work. If there’s a lot going on around me I might notice it, but I haven’t really thought of it as a major problem. Is that what you were asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel an inner sense of restlessness, even when you’re not actively doing something, like a feeling that you can’t quite settle down even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I wouldn’t want to overstate it. It’s more noticeable when I’m supposed to be still. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or sometimes in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot or clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Then I sort of catch myself and think, “Right, settle down.”\n\nIt’s not as if I’m trying to be disruptive. It just feels like my body wants to keep doing something, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like preparing a report or reading something lengthy, even if there aren’t any obvious distractions around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. With school work, if I need to prepare something or read through material, I usually can do it, especially if I know it has to be done.\n\nI mean, I may prefer to break things up a bit rather than sit for hours, but I wouldn’t want to say I can’t focus. That sounds stronger than what I experience, I think. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden feelings of anxiety or panic, like your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're overwhelmed by a sense of fear?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I can get worried or tense like anyone, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I wouldn’t describe it as panic or anything like that.\n\nI don’t really have episodes where my heart is racing or I can’t breathe from fear. At least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I still enjoy teaching, for the most part, and I like spending time with my family. Hobbies too, when I actually make time for them.\n\nOf course I can get tired or feel like I’d rather have a quiet evening, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest or that things don’t give me pleasure anymore. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move is something that other people might notice, like fidgeting, pacing, or an inability to sit still for more than a few moments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it might be noticeable at times, though I hope it’s not too disruptive. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or somewhere like church where I’m meant to be still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around in the seat.\n\nUsually I notice and sort of tell myself, “Settle down,” and then I try to stop. It’s not that I’m jumping up constantly, but yes, I can be a bit fidgety. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt so agitated or restless that other people could tell you were unusually tense, or where you were pacing, fidgeting, or moving around much more than usual in a way that wasn’t normal for you?\n\nPatient: Not really “agitated” in a big way, no… but yes, I suppose there are times people could notice I’m a bit restless.\n\nLike when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or even in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Sometimes it gets enough that I have to sort of remind myself, “Come on, settle down.”\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s extreme, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness or need to move ever caused you any problems with your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me if I’m fidgeting or I seem like I want to move things along. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, but I’ll leave feeling embarrassed, like I didn’t come across well.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it has affected relationships a bit. Not terribly, but enough that I notice it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move has been present most days, and has been going on for at least several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d say every single day, but most days, yes, it’s there in some form.\n\nIt’s been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, I’d say—since my youngest left for college and the house got quieter. I suppose I’ve been more aware of it since then. It’s not constant or anything, but yes, it’s been going on for more than a few weeks. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself starting tasks without thinking them through, interrupting others, or feeling like you're moving or speaking too quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I’m a bit embarrassed to admit it.\n\nIn group discussions, like at book club or in curriculum meetings, I’ll sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. It’s not that I mean to be rude—I really don’t—but the answer or response is already there in my head, and before I’ve properly checked myself, I’ve said it.\n\nI do try to catch it and apologize, but yes, that does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or lacking in energy, even if you’ve had enough rest, and does this happen most days?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a full day, but I wouldn’t say I’m lacking energy most days if I’ve rested.\n\nI haven’t noticed that as a particular problem. If anything, I’m usually more… keyed up than worn out, but tiredness isn’t something I’d point to. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I suppose… yes, a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.

Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move is something that other people might notice, like fidgeting, pacing, or an inability to sit still for more than a few moments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it might be noticeable at times, though I hope it’s not too disruptive. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or somewhere like church where I’m meant to be still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around in the seat.\n\nUsually I notice and sort of tell myself, “Settle down,” and then I try to stop. It’s not that I’m jumping up constantly, but yes, I can be a bit fidgety. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt so agitated or restless that other people could tell you were unusually tense, or where you were pacing, fidgeting, or moving around much more than usual in a way that wasn’t normal for you?\n\nPatient: Not really “agitated” in a big way, no… but yes, I suppose there are times people could notice I’m a bit restless.\n\nLike when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or even in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness or need to move ever caused you any problems with your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me if I’m fidgeting or I seem like I want to move things along. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself starting tasks without thinking them through, interrupting others, or feeling like you're moving or speaking too quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I’m a bit embarrassed to admit it.\n\nIn group discussions, like at book club or in curriculum meetings, I’ll sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. It’s not that I mean to be rude—I really don’t—but the answer or response is already there in my head, and before I’ve properly checked myself, I’ve said it.\n\nI do try to catch it and apologize, but yes, that does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or lacking in energy, even if you’ve had enough rest, and does this happen most days?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I get tired like anyone does, especially after a full day, but I wouldn’t say I’m lacking energy most days if I’ve rested.\n\nI haven’t noticed that as a particular problem. If anything, I’m usually more… keyed up than worn out, but tiredness isn’t something I’d point to. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often lose things, forget tasks, or fail to finish things you start, even if they’re important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me, no.\n\nI mean, I misplace things occasionally—glasses, a pen, that sort of thing—but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it? Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about everyday things, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, or do you feel like it’s hard to control your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in a way that feels out of proportion.\n\nI mean, I worry about the usual things—work, family, whether I’ve forgotten something—but I wouldn’t say it takes over or that I can’t control it. I can usually talk myself through it or put it aside.\n\nSo no, I don’t think that’s really been a problem for me. I don’t think so, not especially.

I mean, if I’m tired I might lose my thread for a moment, or I’ll have to reread an email before replying, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally disorganized or skipping steps in any serious way. With teaching, I’ve always had to keep plans and details straight, so I tend to use lists and notes.

So… I’d say no, not really a major difficulty for me. Is that the sort of distinction you’re asking about?

It just feels like my body wants to keep doing something, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to stay focused on tasks that require sustained mental effort, like preparing a report or reading something lengthy, even if there aren’t any obvious distractions around?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way that really stands out to me. With school work, if I need to prepare something or read through material, I usually can do it, especially if I know it has to be done.\n\nI mean, I may prefer to break things up a bit rather than sit for hours, but I wouldn’t want to say I can’t focus. That sounds stronger than what I experience, I think. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever experience sudden feelings of anxiety or panic, like your heart races, you feel short of breath, or you're overwhelmed by a sense of fear?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure or interest in activities that you used to enjoy, like hobbies, time with family, or even teaching?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move is something that other people might notice, like fidgeting, pacing, or an inability to sit still for more than a few moments?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it might be noticeable at times, though I hope it’s not too disruptive. If I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or somewhere like church where I’m meant to be still, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot or clicking a pen, or shifting around in the seat.\n\nUsually I notice and sort of tell myself, “Settle down,” and then I try to stop. It’s not that I’m jumping up constantly, but yes, I can be a bit fidgety. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had periods where you felt so agitated or restless that other people could tell you were unusually tense, or where you were pacing, fidgeting, or moving around much more than usual in a way that wasn’t normal for you?\n\nPatient: Not really “agitated” in a big way, no… but yes, I suppose there are times people could notice I’m a bit restless.\n\nLike when I’m sitting at my desk grading essays, or even in church, I’ll realize I’m tapping my foot, clicking my pen, or shifting around in the seat. Sometimes it gets enough that I have to sort of remind myself, “Come on, settle down.”\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s extreme, but it does happen. Is that the sort of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness or need to move ever caused you any problems with your work, relationships, or other important areas of your life?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nAt work, in meetings, I can tell people sometimes lose patience with me if I’m fidgeting or I seem like I want to move things along. And in my community—like book club—it’s similar. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable, but I’ll leave feeling embarrassed, like I didn’t come across well.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it has affected relationships a bit. Not terribly, but enough that I notice it. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that this feeling of restlessness or need to move has been present most days, and has been going on for at least several weeks or more?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself starting tasks without thinking them through, interrupting others, or feeling like you're moving or speaking too quickly?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, though I’m a bit embarrassed to admit it.\n\nIn group discussions, like at book club or in curriculum meetings, I’ll sometimes jump in before the other person has quite finished. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s probably been there a long time, honestly… though I wouldn’t have called it a problem back then.

Even when I was a girl, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I had trouble staying in my seat or waiting my turn. And my mother used to tease me—kindly, I think—that before I was ten I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.

So yes, I suppose it does go back to childhood. I hope that answers what you’re asking.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often lose things, forget tasks, or fail to finish things you start, even if they’re important?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that’s a major issue for me, no.\n\nI mean, I misplace things occasionally—glasses, a pen, that sort of thing—but I think that’s fairly normal, isn’t it? And with important tasks, I usually do get them done. I may need a list or a reminder, but I wouldn’t say I often fail to finish important things.\n\nSo… no, not particularly. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about everyday things, even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned, or do you feel like it’s hard to control your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no… not in a way that feels out of proportion.\n\nI mean, I worry about the usual things—work, family, whether I’ve forgotten something—but I wouldn’t say it takes over or that I can’t control it. I can usually talk myself through it or put it aside.\n\nSo no, I don’t think that’s really been a problem for me. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your restlessness or need to move has often been accompanied by feeling overly active or impulsive in ways that are not typical for you, such as talking excessively, having trouble waiting your turn, or acting without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, a bit, though I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nSince the house got quieter after my youngest left for college, I do notice I’m constantly moving from one thing to the next—lesson planning, then laundry, then maybe softball practice, then chopping food for some community event. Even on nights when I’ve told myself, “Right, tonight I’m resting,” I still end up doing something.\n\nI’m not sure I’d call it “overly active” exactly, but I do seem to have a hard time just stopping. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or keep track of details, even when you're trying to focus, like losing your train of thought or skipping steps in a plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not especially.\n\nI mean, if I’m tired I might lose my thread for a moment, or I’ll have to reread an email before replying, but I wouldn’t say I’m generally disorganized or skipping steps in any serious way. With teaching, I’ve always had to keep plans and details straight, so I tend to use lists and notes.\n\nSo… I’d say no, not really a major difficulty for me. Is that the sort of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Would you say these feelings of restlessness and difficulty staying still have been with you for most of your life, going back to when you were a child or teenager, or do you think they started more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably been there a long time, honestly… though I wouldn’t have called it a problem back then.\n\nEven when I was a girl, my report cards would say things like I was bright, but I had trouble staying in my seat or waiting my turn. And my mother used to tease me—kindly, I think—that before I was ten I’d worn a path between my desk and the pencil sharpener because I was up and down so much.\n\nSo yes, I suppose it does go back to childhood. I hope that answers what you’re asking."
